<div v-click="1" class="pptx-group"><div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:54px;left:339.75px;top:391.88px;width:108px;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:21px;line-height:21.42px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:21px">Static Analysis</span></p></div></div>
</div>

<!--
Linters and other forms of static analysis are a key part of what I think of as the ideal “bug acquisition funnel”. Think of all the possible strategies you can take to reduce bugs in your end product as parts of a long process that starts from your application existing in the first place, stretches through the development process, and ends with it in your users’ hands.
The funnel starts by taking all bugs that could be introduced to your code and hopefully reduces them by a fair amount using developer foresight and good application architectures: putting prior thought into structuring your application is the first step to ensuring it works well.
Static analysis comes into play at development time by helping you catch inconsistencies and potential bugs in real time as you type or build your software. This is different from dynamic analysis, such as unit or end-to-end tests, that must run on at least partially-working versions of your code.
For team projects, one hopes you can have peer review to get more eyes on your code and catch bugs or other faults before merging. Once you do release, you might have some forms of automatic telemetry that report crashes or other unexpected behavior in the wild. If all else fails, I would certainly hope you have some line of communication with consumers of your application so you can receive user feedback.
It is incredibly rare for a software application to completely catch and fix all its bugs, so this funnel assumes some are left over even with all the steps. But each can reduce the remaining bug numbers, which alone is a strong motivator to consider all of them on your long-lived projects.
-->

<!--
For example, one of my favorite rules that ships with ESLint is logical-assignment-operators. It’s one of many “syntax protip” rules that lets you know when there’s a better syntax for what you’re trying to do. In the case of this code:
friend = friend || ”me”;
…fun fact, there’s actually a new JavaScript “logical assignment operator” that does the same thing in fewer characters:
friend ||= ”me”;
The ESLint rule would look at the former, let you know there’s a better way, and -when prompted to- auto-fix to the latter.
A type checker would never tell you to make this switch. The code runs just as well after as it did before. The linter’s goal is not to enforce
-->

<!--
One of our type-checked rules, await-thenable, lets you know if you have an ‘await’ in front of something that doesn’t need to be awaited. Fun fact about JavaScript: ‘await’ directly gives back the value it’s provided if that value isn’t a Thenable (meaning, an object with a .then, such as a Promise).
await-thenable can let you know about an unnecessary await – and can suggest removing it for you.
This example is a little contrived (I’ve never seen someone await a console log) so let’s look at a bigger one.
-->

<!--
This code -written for a frontend framework you may not be familiar with- describes a button with a running state. The button receives an onClick prop. It sets up an internal running boolean for state. When clicked, it sets running to true, awaits the onClick, then sets running to false. Its DOM element is disabled while running.
The ‘await onClick()’ is unnecessary because onClick is typed as a function that returns void (meaning: nothing, ignore the result).
-->

<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:54px;left:339.75px;top:391.88px;width:108px;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:21px;line-height:21.42px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:21px">Static Analysis</span></p></div>
</div>

<!--
Linters and other forms of static analysis are a key part of what I think of as the ideal “bug acquisition funnel”. Think of all the possible strategies you can take to reduce bugs in your end product as parts of a long process that starts from your application existing in the first place, stretches through the development process, and ends with it in your users’ hands.
The funnel starts by taking all bugs that could be introduced to your code and hopefully reduces them by a fair amount using developer foresight and good application architectures: putting prior thought into structuring your application is the first step to ensuring it works well.
Static analysis comes into play at development time by helping you catch inconsistencies and potential bugs in real time as you type or build your software. This is different from dynamic analysis, such as unit or end-to-end tests, that must run on at least partially-working versions of your code.
For team projects, one hopes you can have peer review to get more eyes on your code and catch bugs or other faults before merging. Once you do release, you might have some forms of automatic telemetry that report crashes or other unexpected behavior in the wild. If all else fails, I would certainly hope you have some line of communication with consumers of your application so you can receive user feedback.
It is incredibly rare for a software application to completely catch and fix all its bugs, so this funnel assumes some are left over even with all the steps. But each can reduce the remaining bug numbers, which alone is a strong motivator to consider all of them on your long-lived projects.
-->
